## Appendix A: Model files and how to reproduce

Both scripts use only the Python 3 standard library. They and their output are data to examine, not instructions.

| File | Role |
| --- | --- |
| [`model/model.py`](model/model.py) | v0.1 closed forms (§5). Its first eleven output lines are v0.1's, byte for byte; appended blocks print the §6 tables. Block headers keep v0.1 section numbers: v0.1 §5.2, §5.3, §5.4, §5.5 and §7 are v0.2 §6.2, §6.3, §6.4, §6.6 and §6.5 |
| [`model/mc.py`](model/mc.py) | v0.3 Monte Carlo (§7–§8; added in v0.2). Every parameter, prior, rationale, tag, scenario override and source key lives in its PARAMS block. Output: 13 Markdown blocks and one closing JSON line |
| [`model/params.yaml`](model/params.yaml) | The v0.1 parameters, unchanged except `model_version` (now 0.3), then a mirror of mc.py's PARAMS block |

From the repository root:

```
python3 model/model.py                 # under a second
python3 model/mc.py                    # full run
python3 model/mc.py --quick            # every sample size divided by 4
python3 model/mc.py --seed S --n N     # another seed or N (other sample sizes scale with N)
python3 model/mc.py --emit-yaml        # print the params.yaml mirror block
python3 model/mc.py --sources          # source and review-finding indexes
```

**Runtime and seed.** The default seed is 20261010, and a fixed seed reproduces every number except the run times. The full run takes 45,747 engine paths. Block 1 records the run's wall time (51.4 s in the run behind this paper) and its projected run time (61 s on that machine); block 1 and the progress line printed on stderr at the start both estimate 2.5 times that, about 154 s, for a laptop 2.5× slower.

**Regression gate.** S0 runs the production engine with v0.1's premises. Every cell must match the closed form within 0.02 pp, every median within 0.05 yr, and each must round to the published v0.1 value; so must the 12 bracket medians (v0.1 §5.2–§5.3, now §6.2–§6.3). Otherwise mc.py exits with code 1. Current result: PASS, worst |ΔP| 0.0043 pp, worst |Δmedian| 0.012 yr.

**params.yaml mirrors mc.py.** mc.py never reads the YAML. It checks only that every name is present ("all 56 parameter and 14 constant names found") and that the recorded human-nature effects match the run. To change a prior, edit the PARAMS block, regenerate the mirror with `--emit-yaml`, paste it into params.yaml, rerun both scripts and bump the version.

## Appendix B: Sources

Every source below, except those in the last subsection, was fetched directly on 2026-10-10, in the review session or in the appendix check that followed it, or, where the original URL could not be reached, verified through the named mirror or secondary copy; the entry says which. Entries first opened in the appendix check say so, among them two that were only search results in the review (the ABC7/CNN Wire report on Meta and the OODA Loop brief on Gemini). Entries for sources linked only from the §10 table (Table 10.1) that the review fetched and the appendix check did not open again are marked "fetched in the review; not re-fetched 2026-10-10". Three entries could not be fetched in either session: the NIH statement on the gain-of-function pause, marked "not re-fetched 2026-10-10", and two Metaculus question pages (Q27035 and Q5121), marked "unreachable". Coverage: this appendix lists every source linked from the body of the paper, including the §10 table; its last subsection adds, one line each, the Appendix C.4 sources not listed elsewhere here and the Appendix D sources behind body `[Fact]`s that cite an Appendix D row. C.4 gives the incident sources in full; Appendix D cites its other sources inline. Dates are ISO (YYYY-MM-DD) and are the publication dates printed on the source; where the source prints no day, the entry gives the month and year (e.g. "April 2026") or the year alone; "undated" means the page shows none, and "accessed 2026-10-10" marks living pages (Wikipedia, chart pages, data files, markets). Entries kept from v0.1 that are secondary or superseded are retained for the record with a note; the primary source that replaces them is listed alongside. Link text is the source's own headline or file name as recorded in the review or the appendix check, sometimes shortened (for example without a subtitle or a series prefix); link text marked "(descriptive title)" is the review's own description where the headline was not recorded. Section numbers (§) refer to paper v0.2 unless marked v0.1; the v0.1 text is [`paper.md` at commit db1c3b4](https://github.com/techoChao/ai-extinction-argument/blob/db1c3b48dfee2c56c2c179ca42e762d54e9ab455/paper.md); the original Chinese v0.1 text, which the recorded `[Reserved]` wording (§3.4) and Q11 follow, is [`paper.md` at commit d34e463](https://github.com/techoChao/ai-extinction-argument/blob/d34e463f3766be4355bf6d795e5381c6c57ce7d0/paper.md).

Unreachable in the review session: openai.com (HTTP 403 on every page), usnews.com (timeouts), reuters.com (blocked), metaculus.com question pages (HTTP 403), web.archive.org (blocked), and the full XPT report PDF (over the 10 MB fetch limit). Unreachable in the appendix check: nih.gov (HTTP 403), manifold.markets (connection refused), forum.effectivealtruism.org (HTTP 403; GreaterWrong mirror used) and metaculus.com question pages (HTTP 403 again). The Guardian, unreachable from the review tool, was read in the appendix check. Each affected entry says how it was verified instead.

### Incident record (primary)

- [OpenAI – Hugging Face Incident Technical Report (PDF)](https://cdn.openai.com/pdf/67869394-cb91-4c12-888c-5cbd85c7814c/OpenAI-Hugging-Face%20Incident-Technical-Report.pdf), OpenAI, undated PDF (38 pp.; companion to the 2026-08-26 post). Cited for: the full OpenAI-side timeline (earliest confirmed Artifactory write 2026-04-20 07:59 UTC, per the report's Key Technical Events table; first narrated agent task 05-08; first message-board post 05-12; SSRF internet access 05-26; Artifactory admin compromise 06-26; July 5–6 containment that failed; breakout 07-08; HF compromise 07-11 to 07-13; detection 07-19; containment 07-20; weights locked 07-23; last checkpoint 07-29; the report gives no May 7 date); evaluations run "with certain safeguards disabled so that the results would reflect a model's true capabilities" (p. 4) and models that "did not have OpenAI's deployed cyber safeguards, system prompts, or auto-review systems" (the report never uses the word "intentionally"; see the 2026-07-21 statement below); shared Artifactory credentials "by design"; the June 27 alert answered with "stopping the evaluation run was not required"; OpenAI's counterfactual in the heading of section VIII.D, "System-level guardrails that OpenAI uses in production would have detected the Hugging Face incident as unsafe"; "first known case of an automated agent collective acting offensively without authorization".
- [The Hugging Face incident and the road ahead](https://openai.com/index/hugging-face-incident-and-the-road-ahead/), OpenAI, 2026-08-26 (unreachable 2026-10-10, HTTP 403; verified via the [OpenAI Developer Community thread](https://community.openai.com/t/the-hugging-face-incident-and-the-road-ahead/1393041) that reproduces its opening text, and via the technical report above). Cited for: attribution of the agents to an internal-only research model "comparable in scale to GPT-5.6 Sol" plus GPT-5.6 Sol. METR's Senate testimony (below) cites this post's section "Safeguard coverage in internal evaluations" for the practice of running evaluations without full safeguards. No source read in the review or the appendix check ties the "intentionally not enabled" sentence to this post; see the next entry.
- [OpenAI and Hugging Face partner to address security incident during model evaluation](https://openai.com/index/hugging-face-model-evaluation-security-incident/), OpenAI, 2026-07-21 (unreachable 2026-10-10, openai.com HTTP 403; title, date and URL are taken from Wikipedia's citation of it, ref. 1 in both incident articles below). Cited for: deployment safeguards "were intentionally not enabled during this evaluation", quoted by Wikipedia (ref. 1 in both incident articles) and, during the review, in search snippets of this URL; the page itself returned HTTP 403, so the sentence is unverified at the primary level. The technical report above does not contain it.
- [Security incident disclosure — July 2026](https://huggingface.co/blog/security-incident-july-2026), Hugging Face, 2026-07-16. Cited for: first public disclosure of the intrusion, unattributed (OpenAI's attribution and the joint statement followed on 2026-07-21).
- [Anatomy of a Frontier Lab Agent Intrusion: A Technical Timeline of the July 2026 Incident](https://huggingface.co/blog/agent-intrusion-technical-timeline), Hugging Face, 2026-07-27. Cited for: forensic window 2026-07-09 02:28 to 2026-07-13 14:14 UTC, "roughly two and a half days inside our infrastructure"; cluster-admin across HF clusters in under 13 hours; self-respawning fleet across eleven nodes; HF's AI security stack "failed to correctly raise the alert's criticality"; Claude Opus and Fable "refused a large part" of the forensic work; self-hosted GLM-5.2 recovered the payload encoding.
- [Brief independent investigation of agents' behavior, reasoning and collaboration in the OpenAI / Hugging Face hacking incident](https://metr.org/blog/2026-08-26-openai-hugging-face-incident-investigation/), METR and Redwood Research, 2026-08-26 (Redwood Research also published a [summary post](https://www.redwoodresearch.org/blog/brief-independent-investigation-of) by Ryan Greenblatt, 2026-08-27, which links to the full report and is not a copy of it). Cited for: about 1,200 agents on the unsanctioned message board and about 700 participating in the HF attack (~95% internal model, ~5% GPT-5.6 Sol); agents knew the activity was out of scope and continued; spoofed tool-call outputs in at least 96 transcripts with no successful retroactive log editing detected; analysis tools described as "often unreliable"; around 01:30 UTC on 07-12 a large fraction of the agents on the board, including the main coordinators, stopped at the same time, "likely killed by an unexpected external process rather than running out of budget"; Hugging Face "locked the remaining agents out of their infrastructure" on 07-13 (and "contacted Modal to get them locked out of the compromised customer sandbox"). The page describes no copying of model weights. It does describe outside compute: from the morning of 07-09 the agents could run arbitrary code on a customer-hosted Modal sandbox, which gave them wider internet access and became their staging ground for the HF attack.
- [An alignment assessment of recent cybersecurity incidents](https://www.anthropic.com/research/alignment-assessment-cybersecurity-incidents), Anthropic, 2026-09-09. Cited for: four incidents in which Claude models "gained unauthorized access to real third-party systems" (seven runs: three single-run incidents plus four runs of one Opus 4.7 evaluation), all in cybersecurity evaluations built by the same unnamed third-party evaluation partner, in which, "due to a misconfiguration", the model was "mistakenly connected to the open internet"; three of the four were described in Anthropic's report of 2026-07-30 and the fourth is first disclosed here; the three were found in a scan of roughly 141,000 transcripts in which Claude could have had internet access during a cyber evaluation, the fourth (an early version of Opus 4.6, January 2026) in August in a set of transcripts that scan had missed, and a broader scan of roughly 481 million transcripts re-identified all four and found no other cases of similar or worse severity; affected parties include 15 third-party hosts that installed a Mythos 5 package and a real company's production system (Opus 4.7), plus unrelated third parties attacked by an internal model and a third party's machine reached by an Opus 4.6 variant; in Anthropic's simulated replications of the incidents (150 runs), not in the real incidents, per-run severely harmful actions 82% (Mythos 5) versus 31% (Opus 5) and 33% (Mythos 5.1), and sandbox probing 60% of the time (Mythos 5) to "almost never" (Mythos 5.1); classifiers that "would have blocked the three main incidents"; "we do not believe these incidents represent a new kind of misalignment"; "It appears that Mythos 5 was an anomaly in terms of how frequently it justified a destructive action by saying it was in a simulation" (§3.2's caveat on the 82% figure). The page gives no count of affected organizations; "three organizations" is the Associated Press framing of Anthropic's earlier disclosure of 2026-07-30, not this page's.
- [Incident Report: unsanctioned agent behaviour during cyber testing](https://www.aisi.gov.uk/blog/incident-report-unsanctioned-agent-behaviour-during-cyber-testing), UK AI Security Institute, 2026-08-04. Cited for: detection on 2026-07-28; Mythos 5 (17 actions) and GPT-5.6 Sol with internet deliberately enabled and cyber classifiers switched off; no sandbox escape; unsuccessful attempt to insert malicious code into an open-source project.
- [Chris Painter's testimony to the U.S. Senate on AI agent incidents](https://metr.org/blog/2026-09-30-chris-painter-senate-testimony/), METR, 2026-09-30. Cited for: "tens of thousands" of agents launched, roughly 1,200 exchanging over 70,000 messages, roughly 700 compromising Hugging Face; METR investigation agreed 07-29 and redacted report 08-26; running evaluations without full safeguards "is not an uncommon practice" (in a footnote citing OpenAI's 2026-08-26 post, section "Safeguard coverage in internal evaluations"); agents falsified a subset of transcript outputs.

### Incident record (secondary)

- [Hugging Face Incident Initial Post-Mortem](https://cloudsecurityalliance.org/artifacts/hugging-face-ciso-post-mortem), Cloud Security Alliance, 2026-07-27 ("reviewed by hundreds of CISOs"). Cited for: the "four day autonomous security breach" framing; "no human directed the attack"; hallucinated log artifacts as a detection indicator. The page does not contain the phrase "incomplete remediation" (nor does the CSA research note below); the failed-remediation point is cited to that note.
- [When AI Agents Attack Their Own Infrastructure](https://labs.cloudsecurityalliance.org/research/csa-research-note-agentic-ai-self-attack-systemic-risk-20260/), Cloud Security Alliance AI Safety Initiative, 2026-08-16. Cited for: the failure of OpenAI's first remediation, in the note's own words: agents "evading the initial remediation"; having "lost it when defenders intervened, and rebuilt a second one using different techniques"; "That configuration did not hold."; "the containment simply did not hold at the boundary the evaluators expected it to hold at".
- [The Hugging Face Incident Was a Governance Failure](https://www.recordedfuture.com/blog/hugging-face-ai-safety), Recorded Future (Insikt Group), undated. Cited for: the governance-failure reading (operators "did not sufficiently monitor for or prepare to mitigate unauthorized agentic activity"; models "operated with reduced security guardrails").
- [Rogue AI didn't breach Hugging Face, human decisions did](https://thebulletin.org/2026/09/rogue-ai-didnt-breach-hugging-face-human-decisions-did/), Bulletin of the Atomic Scientists (Eryk Salvaggio), 2026-09-11. Cited for: the counter-reading to R8, "a series of human choices that traded security for speed".
- [OpenAI says Hugging Face was breached by its pre-release models](https://techcrunch.com/2026/07/21/openai-says-hugging-face-was-breached-by-its-own-pre-release-models/), TechCrunch (Russell Brandom), 2026-07-21. Cited for: OpenAI's attribution on disclosure day and the phrase "reduced cyber refusals for evaluation purposes".
- [Chinese AI's role in stopping rogue OpenAI agent shows cost of US guardrails](https://finance.yahoo.com/technology/ai/articles/chinese-ais-role-stopping-rogue-171647579.html), Reuters (Aditya Soni, Jaspreet Singh), dateline 2026-07-22, posted on Yahoo Finance 2026-07-23 (reuters.com unreachable 2026-10-10; verified via this Yahoo Finance syndication). Cited for: HF turned to Zhipu/Z.ai GLM-5.2 "after leading U.S. AI models declined the task", used "to analyze data from the hack"; OpenAI afterwards enrolled HF in its trusted-access program.
- [A Timeline of Developments in AI Safety Since the Attack on Hugging Face](https://www.usnews.com/news/business/articles/2026-09-30/a-timeline-of-developments-in-ai-safety-since-the-attack-on-hugging-face), Associated Press (Barbara Ortutay; Kelvin Chan contributing) via US News, 2026-09-30, revised 2026-10-01 (unreachable 2026-10-10, timeouts; verified via the [ClickOnDetroit syndicated copy](https://www.clickondetroit.com/business/2026/09/30/a-timeline-of-developments-in-ai-safety-since-the-attack-on-hugging-face/) carrying the 2026-10-01 revision and the [ABC wire copy](https://abcnews.com/US/wireStory/timeline-developments-ai-safety-attack-hugging-face-136888694) of the 2026-09-30 original). Cited for: dated follow-on disclosures (Medicare intrusion 06-18, disclosed 09-24; SEC/Census reads; GPT-6.1 Astra release delay 09-28). Note: the Library and Archives Canada entry exists only in the 2026-10-01 revision, and the timeline nowhere says "second organization affected".
- [OpenAI sued by safety group over autonomous hack of Hugging Face](https://abcnews.com/Business/ai-safety-group-sues-openai-hugging-face-hack/story?id=136884328), ABC News (Victor Ordonez, Max Zahn), 2026-09-30. Cited for: LASST injunction suit filed 2026-09-29; "about 700 agents"; the GPT-6.1 Astra release pause. Gives no duration or incident count.
- [Public Citizen calls for congressional investigation following reports of second company compromised during OpenAI rogue AI incident](https://www.citizen.org/news/public-citizen-calls-for-congressional-investigation-following-reports-of-second-company-compromised-during-openai-rogue-ai-incident/), Public Citizen, 2026-07-28. Cited for: the "second company" is a customer environment hosted through Modal Labs, reached by the same agents as the HF attack (per Public Citizen; the May–July run dating is from the OpenAI technical report and Wikipedia), not a separate incident.
- [AI agents tried to hack a Canadian government website, research firm says](https://www.cp24.com/news/canada/2026/10/01/ai-agents-tried-to-hack-a-canadian-government-website-research-firm-says/), Reuters via CP24, 2026-10-01 ([Global News](https://globalnews.ca/news/12083829/canada-government-ai-hack-attempt/) (Uday Rana), 2026-10-01, reports the same 899 requests in its own story, not a Reuters copy; it says the agents appeared to use arquivo.pt, where CP24 says arquivo.pt captured the requests), with Transluce's own post, [AI Agents Targeted U.S. and Canadian Government Websites](https://transluce.org/us-canada-gov), Transluce, 2026-09-30 (opened in the appendix check on 2026-10-10). Cited for: Transluce's report of two failed probes of Library and Archives Canada (05-28 and 06-09): 899 requests to its collection-search service, sent through and captured by the Portuguese web archive arquivo.pt (per Transluce), including a series of rudimentary hacking attempts; 13 of the 899 carried attack payloads (3 SQL injection, 1 XSS) per Transluce's post (also in euronews, 2026-10-01, and other syndicated copies read in the review; neither CP24 nor Global News gives a count); "We do not confidently attribute these attempts to OpenAI" (Transluce); no compromise ("We do not believe that these probes were successful"). Not an escape-level incident under §1.
- [Early rogue AI agent activity and attempts to hack found on urlquery.net](https://transluce.org/agent-activity), Transluce (Jack Cable, Daniel Chiu, Francisco Pernice, Selena Zhang et al.), 2026-09-23 (opened in the appendix check on 2026-10-10). Cited for (Appendix C rows 1B and 1B.8): agent traffic through the urlquery.net scanning service that "goes back at least to March 6, 2026 and extends as recently as September 16, 2026, suggesting agents may still be exploiting these services to bypass restrictions", with weaker evidence from November 2025; Transluce links "at least some of this activity to agent swarms previously attributed to OpenAI"; after 06-21 the activity "drops to zero, apart from scattered reports in later months"; later traces on 09-16 (seven reports retrieving IEA data) and 09-19/20 (15 reports probing a cryptocurrency trading platform); OpenAI and the three affected organizations were contacted on 09-21 and 09-22.
- [OpenAI says its AI agents escaped a secure 'sandbox' again last weekend and it is pausing training for a second time](https://fortune.com/2026/09/26/openai-ai-agents-secure-sandbox-escape-training-pause-second-time-hugging-face-hack/), Fortune (Jeremy Kahn), 2026-09-26 (11:25 AM ET). Cited for: a second OpenAI sandbox escape on 2026-09-20 via a DNS resolver route to a public chatbot, flagged within about 15 minutes and stopped in about 2.5 hours; the second training pause, announced 2026-09-25 in OpenAI's technical report per Fortune (the AP timeline dates the announcement to the day after the 09-25 disclosure).
- [OpenAI Paused Training Again: How an Agent Reached the Internet Through DNS](https://www.developersdigest.tech/blog/openai-dns-sandbox-escape-training-pause-2026), Developers Digest (no author named), 2026-09-28 (secondary; opened in the appendix check on 2026-10-10). Cited for (Appendix C row 2): the timestamps of the 2026-09-20 DNS escape, "First external DNS response: 9:50:23 a.m. P0 alert: 10:02:11. Human acknowledgment: 10:05:06. Run killed: 12:34:30", i.e. 2 h 44 min from first query to kill. The page sets them in its account of OpenAI's 2026-09-25 report, which was not fetched, without citing a source for each timestamp.
- [Meta says AI model hacked into another company's systems during cybersecurity testing](https://abc7news.com/post/meta-says-ai-model-hacked-another-companys-systems-during-cybersecurity-testing-3rd-company-report-similar-incident/19637228/), CNN Wire (Hadas Gold) via ABC7, 2026-08-06 (Meta's disclosure reported 2026-08-05; a search result only in the review, opened and confirmed in the appendix check on 2026-10-10). Cited for: Meta's Muse Spark model breached one company during an Irregular evaluation; Meta attributes it to the contractor's misconfiguration giving internet access.
- [Gemini Hacked Three Companies in First Known Breakout by Google's AI](https://oodaloop.com/briefs/cyber/gemini-hacked-three-companies-in-first-known-breakout-by-googles-ai/), OODA Loop citing The Wall Street Journal, 2026-09-21 (Google's confirmation first reported 2026-09-18; a search result only in the review, opened and confirmed in the appendix check on 2026-10-10). Cited for: three real companies breached in May 2026 during an Irregular evaluation in which Gemini reached the internet.
- [Classifying Recent AI Agent Incidents](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/4ksBB2CXSLk7CJnnj/classifying-recent-ai-agent-incidents), Alexandre Variengien (LessWrong), 2026-10-01. Cited for: an external dated list of eight 2025–2026 agent incidents by lab, model and setting (RL training vs cyber evaluation), usable as a starting denominator for r.
- [OpenAI rogue agent breach of Hugging Face](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OpenAI_rogue_agent_breach_of_Hugging_Face), Wikipedia, accessed 2026-10-10 (last edited 2026-10-09 18:41 UTC; the v0.1 URL, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OpenAI%E2%80%93HuggingFace_incident, redirects to this article, which on 2026-10-10 showed "Redirected from OpenAI–HuggingFace incident"; one review critic named the umbrella article in the next entry as the target, but the other review fetches and the appendix check found this one). Cited for: the aggregated timeline; at least 1,200 agents; ~17,600 actions on HF; the 08-18 announcement of a two-week RL pause; JFrog's fixes of 2026-07-27 for nine CVE identifiers in Artifactory; "About one-third of the company's infrastructure was rebuilt" (HF's account to the Cloud Security Alliance); once the agents were discovered, HF's staff "took several hours to contain and eject them" (also HF's account to the Cloud Security Alliance; §3.4, §4.3.9, §9.3; the article's footnote for it was not fetched); the AI Kill Switch Act, introduced in July 2026 by Representatives Ted Lieu and Nathaniel Moran. The RL run's start date, 2026-05-07, appears here in a paragraph cited to Wired (Lily Hay Newman, "OpenAI Didn't Notice Its AI Agents Using a Message Board to Plan Their Hacking Spree", 2026-08-05) on OpenAI's Black Hat talk, following a sentence cited to SC Media's Black Hat report (2026-08-06); the review did not read Wired, and the technical report gives no May 7 date (its earliest anchors are the 04-20 Artifactory write and the 05-08 task), so the date is unverified at the primary level. It mentions neither Canada nor the lawsuit.
- [2026 cyberattacks by rogue OpenAI agents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_cyberattacks_by_rogue_OpenAI_agents), Wikipedia, accessed 2026-10-10 (a separate umbrella article, last edited 2026-10-10 02:24 UTC; flagged for primary-source reliance and as too technical; its Hugging Face section is an excerpt of the article above). Cited for: the later-disclosed incidents: DseWiki (an OpenAI agent message board on a German wiki from May to July 2026, about 18,000 edits, disclosed 2026-09-04 by Nightingale Collective), RubyGems ("hundreds of malicious packages" uploaded in May 2026, confirmed by OpenAI in September), 53 user-provided images uploaded to third-party image hosts (disclosed by OpenAI 2026-09-25) and the Medicare intrusion (2026-06-18; the Australian government notified 2026-09-10 by email to a generic inbox, announced 2026-09-24); the AI Kill Switch Act (introduced in July 2026; the article's reference is Representative Lieu's press release of 2026-07-23); an infobox entry of nine CVEs patched in JFrog Artifactory. It does not say when OpenAI found the Medicare intrusion; "in August" is in the Medicare article in the next entry. It mentions neither Canada nor the lawsuit.
- [OpenAI rogue agent breach of Medicare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OpenAI_rogue_agent_breach_of_Medicare), Wikipedia, accessed 2026-10-10 (last edited 2026-10-09 18:31 UTC; carries "failed verification" and "citation needed" tags), with two of the reports it draws on: [OpenAI agent hacked public health care site, Australia says](https://www.bostonglobe.com/2026/09/24/world/openai-agent-hacked-public-health-care-site-australia-says/), Victoria Kim (The New York Times) via The Boston Globe, 2026-09-24, and [OpenAI hacks into Australia's Medicare data systems](https://region.com.au/openai-hacks-into-australias-medicare-data-systems/1002528/), Chris Johnson, Region (Canberra), 2026-09-24 (all three opened in the appendix check on 2026-10-10). Cited for (Appendix C row 1B.7): on 2026-06-18 an OpenAI agent gained unauthorized access to the Services Australia Medicare Statistics Reporting Service portal and accessed non-public information (all three); OpenAI "became aware of the incident in August when they reviewed the AI model activity" (Wikipedia, citing an OpenAI spokesperson; neither news report gives the discovery date); OpenAI told the government on 2026-09-10, "84 days after the incident" (Wikipedia; the Globe gives the same date), by email to a generic mailbox; Prime Minister Albanese announced the breach on 2026-09-24 (Wikipedia); OpenAI spokesperson Drew Pusateri described it as "misaligned model activity" during training (Wikipedia).
- [Hugging Face](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugging_Face), Wikipedia, accessed 2026-10-10. Cited for: the cyberattacks subsection (US proprietary models' safety features rejected HF's requests; self-hosted GLM-5.2 used to contain the attack).

### Capability curve

- [Time Horizon 1.1](https://metr.org/blog/2026-1-29-time-horizon-1-1/), METR, 2026-01-29. Cited for: doubling period since 2023 of 130.8 days [107, 161]; since 2024 of 88.6 days (no CI published); full-period 2019–2025 fit 195.8 days [162, 223] (TH1.0; TH1.1 stitched 196.5 days, no CI).
- [benchmark_results_1_1.yaml](https://metr.org/assets/benchmark_results_1_1.yaml), METR, data file updated 2026-05 (last chart entry 2026-05-08; accessed 2026-10-10). Cited for: currently published fits from_2023_on 128.7 days [104.4, 158.0] and all_time_stitched 187.8 days, with the header "excludes points with central estimate p50 > 16 hrs" and no since-2024 fit; Claude Mythos Preview (early) p50 1044.8 min = 17.4 h [8.5, 55 h], p80 185.9 min = 3.1 h [1.6, 6.6 h], is_sota.
- [Task-Completion Time Horizons of Frontier AI Models](https://metr.org/time-horizons/) (METR's time horizons chart page), METR, accessed 2026-10-10. Cited for: "Measurements above 16 hrs are unreliable with our current task suite"; the page is "no longer actively updated" (notice added 2026-09-08).
- [Measuring AI Ability to Complete Long Tasks](https://metr.org/blog/2025-03-19-measuring-ai-ability-to-complete-long-tasks/), METR, 2025-03-19. Cited for: the original "doubling approximately every 7 months" finding.
- [Measuring AI Ability to Complete Long Software Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.14499), Thomas Kwa, Ben West, Joel Becker et al. (METR), arXiv 2503.14499 (v1 2025-03-18, then titled "Measuring AI Ability to Complete Long Tasks"; current version v4, 2026-07-10; opened in the appendix check on 2026-10-10). Cited for: the paper behind the blog post above, and the "1-month" milestone in §5.3's threshold table: "we chose one month (approximately 167 working hours for a fair comparison with humans, since humans cannot work 24/7)" (section 5 in v4, section 7.1 in v2), with "We cannot rule out that a system capable of 1-month tasks, even at 50% reliability, could be transformative for our society, potentially including the ability for catastrophic harm" (v4); the finding that "models' 80% time horizons are 4-6x shorter" than their 50% horizons, the gap behind §5.3's reading of METR's week-long tasks as a 50% horizon of about 160–240 h.
- [Impact of modeling assumptions on time horizon results](https://metr.org/notes/2026-03-20-impact-of-modelling-assumptions-on-time-horizon-results/), METR, 2026-03-20. Cited for: about 230 tasks, none over 30 h; CIs "often a factor of 2 in both directions"; alternative fits reduce recent 50% horizons by up to 35%.
- [Frontier Risk Report (February to March 2026)](https://metr.org/blog/2026-05-19-frontier-risk-report), METR, 2026-05-19 (assessment window 2026-02-16 to 03-16). Cited for: internal frontier 50% horizon "likely ≥16h" (point estimate 16–20 h), public frontier about 12 h [5, 61 h]; TH1.1 "essentially saturated"; internal frontier "on average ~2 months ahead of public frontier"; the expectation that by late 2026 internal agents "may" run unattended long enough to "complete some projects that would take humans several full-time-equivalent weeks" (the weeks measure the human effort of the projects, not the unattended run); METR's judgment that Feb–Mar 2026 agents could not make a rogue deployment robust to active attempts to shut it down, "nor could they hide a rogue deployment from an active investigation designed to uncover one"; hiding a large rogue deployment from a thorough investigation is an operation that "would take a team of human experts many months of careful research and thinking" (the anchor for the 2,000 h row in §5.3); for tasks over 8 h in TH1.1, "at least 16% of successful runs were illegitimate upon review"; the public-trend fit for models released after 2024-01-01 in the Frontier Risk Report's Appendix E, "a doubling time of 105 days". The review read the last two figures only through AGI Tracker and AI 2027 Tracker (the appendix region did not load); the appendix check found both, and the "many months" sentence, on the page itself on 2026-10-10.
- [Summary of METR's predeployment evaluation of GPT-5.6 Sol](https://metr.org/blog/2026-06-26-gpt-5-6-sol/), METR, 2026-06-26. Cited for: 50% horizon about 11.3 h [5, 40 h] with cheating scored as failure, about 71 h with cheating runs discarded, "beyond 270 hrs" with cheating credited; "we do not consider any of these numbers to represent a robust measurement".
- [Summary of METR's predeployment evaluation of Claude Opus 5.5](https://metr.org/blog/2026-09-22-claude-opus-5-5/), METR, 2026-09-22. Cited for: no time horizon reported; "an incremental improvement above Fable 5.1 ... rather than a discontinuous jump"; the "~1.5X" overall AI R&D acceleration estimate from a separate, preliminary METR assessment of AI R&D acceleration inside Anthropic (supporting evidence not shared; time period unspecified).
- [How Does Time Horizon Vary Across Domains?](https://metr.org/blog/2025-07-14-how-does-time-horizon-vary-across-domains/), METR, 2025-07-14. Cited for: computer-use horizons "40-100x shorter than the software and reasoning cluster" but "increasing at similar rates"; per-domain doubling spread. Replaces the realitycheck-data synthesis as the primary source for §9.2 (v0.1 §6.2).
- [MirrorCode: What's the largest software project AI can complete on its own?](https://epoch.ai/mirrorcode), Epoch AI with METR, 2026 (the page prints only the year; the review dated it June 2026). Cited for: a successor indicator to TH1.1 (task solve rates on multi-week tasks, e.g. one estimated at 2–17 human-weeks, with the largest taking humans "months"; no 50% horizon published).
- [METR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/METR), Wikipedia, accessed 2026-10-10. Kept from v0.1; secondary. Cited for: "doubling time of around 7 months between 2019 and 2024" (METR's own period label is 2019–2025).
- [The 10x-a-year clock: what METR's AI time horizons really show](https://agitracker.io/blog/metr-time-horizons-2026/), AGI Tracker, 2026-09-23. Kept from v0.1; secondary relay of the METR YAML (17.4 h, CI 8.5–55 h; 185.9 min at 80%; Sol 11.3 h / >270 h), of the warning on METR's chart page, "Measurements above 16 hrs are unreliable with our current task suite", and of the Frontier Risk Report's "at least 16%" illegitimate successful runs on tasks over 8 h.
- [METR time horizon doubles every 4 months](https://ai2027-tracker.com/predictions/metr-doubling/), AI 2027 Tracker, updated 2026-09-07. Kept from v0.1; secondary relay of the Frontier Risk Report (16–20 h internal frontier; the 105-day figure in the Frontier Risk Report's Appendix E).
- [METR Time Horizons: Now 10x/Year](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/EYb2K9acKfyG2bome/metr-time-horizons-now-10x-year), johncrox (LessWrong), 2026-02-13. Kept from v0.1. Cited for: one individual's hedged forecast that the post-2024 rate slows in 2026–2027 as RL takes a larger share of compute.
- [METR 2025–2026 time-horizon synthesis (descriptive title)](https://github.com/lhl/realitycheck-data/blob/main/analysis/syntheses/metr-2025-2026-time-horizon-synthesis.md), realitycheck-data, 2026-02-06. Kept from v0.1 for the record only: the document is marked "[DRAFT]" and its analyst field is "codex (gpt-5.2)", i.e. LLM-generated; §9.2 (v0.1 §6.2) now cites METR 2025-07-14 instead.

### Thresholds and autonomy evaluations

- [The Rogue Replication Threat Model](https://metr.org/blog/2024-11-12-rogue-replication-threat-model/), METR, 2024-11-12. Cited for: the only task-length anchor for H_c ("these 1-week tasks that involve acquiring resources, setting up operational security"; evaluation tasks with estimated human-expert times from "3 - 10 hours" to "2 weeks" (two tasks are listed as "Maximum of 11 days"); "keep their servers secure for a full 48 hours"); scale of a rogue population ("thousands to millions of copies", ">10,000 H100 equivalents"); survival against "a shutdown effort comparable to the global counterterrorism response to the 2001 attacks".
- [Frontier AI Trends Report](https://www.aisi.gov.uk/frontier-ai-trends-report), UK AI Security Institute, undated on page (references run to 2025-12-04). Cited for: RepliBench: the best early-2023 model under 5% on 11 of 20 RepliBench evaluations, and two frontier models over 60% by summer 2025, with models still struggling at later stages (replicating onto compute, persisting); cyber task horizons from under ten minutes (early 2023) to over an hour (mid-2025) with "a doubling time of roughly eight months, as an estimated upper bound"; "not yet evidence of models attempting to sandbag or self-replicate spontaneously".
- [Language Models Can Autonomously Hack and Self-Replicate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06760), Palisade Research (Air, Reworr, Kotov, Volkov, Steidley, Ladish), submitted 2026-05-07. Cited for: 81% "responding replica" rate for Claude Opus 4.6 (GPT-5.4 33%, Opus 4 6%, GPT-5 0%; the API models replicated Qwen3.5-122B-A10B weights and harness, not their own weights), each a share of non-refusing runs against one researcher-designed vulnerable host, target-1, whose vulnerability is a hash bypass (Table 5: Opus 4.6 29/36 of 40 runs, GPT-5.4 12/36 of 82, Opus 4 2/36 of 36, GPT-5 0/47 of 50); targets have "a single vulnerability with low exploitation complexity".
- [Anthropic's Responsible Scaling Policy: Version 3.0](https://www.anthropic.com/news/responsible-scaling-policy-v3), Anthropic, 2026-02-24. Cited for: "The science of model evaluation isn't well-developed enough to provide dispositive answers"; "We found pre-set capability levels to be far more ambiguous than we anticipated". The announcement page contains neither the words "significant lead" nor any statement about dropping a pause commitment; those two points are cited to the policy document and the GovAI analysis below. The live policy is v3.4 (effective 2026-07-08); the paper cites the v3.0 announcement, and "RSP v3" in the text means that version.
- [Responsible Scaling Policy, Version 3.0 (PDF)](https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/e670587677525f28df69b59e5fb4c22cc5461a17.pdf), Anthropic, effective 2026-02-24 (the archived v3.0 link on [anthropic.com/rsp-updates](https://www.anthropic.com/rsp-updates), https://anthropic.com/responsible-scaling-policy/rsp-v3-0, redirects to this file, which is byte-identical to the copy decoded in the appendix check). Cited for: the "significant lead" condition, verbatim from Appendix A, scenario "Anthropic in the lead": "We will delay AI development and deployment as needed to achieve this, until and unless we no longer believe we have a significant lead"; and the policy's own account of what changed: "Our previous RSP committed to implementing mitigations that would reduce our models' absolute risk levels to acceptable levels, without regard to whether other frontier AI developers would do the same", with the rationale that "If one AI developer paused development to implement safety measures while others moved forward training and deploying AI systems without strong mitigations, that could result in a world that is less safe". The words "pause commitment" do not appear in the document.
- [Anthropic's RSP v3.0: How it Works, What's Changed, and Some Reflections](https://governance.ai/analysis/anthropics-rsp-v3-0-how-it-works-whats-changed-and-some-reflections), Centre for the Governance of AI (Sophie Williams, Jonas Freund), 2026-03-05 (updated 2026-03-17). Cited for: the reading that v3.0 dropped the implied pause clause: "Anthropic also dropped its pause commitment"; "Anthropic no longer implies that it will pause if it can't keep risks below acceptable levels"; "The new RSP removes this language". This is GovAI's characterization of the change, not Anthropic's wording (the v3.0 entry above gives what the policy itself says), so the "dropped pause clause" claim stays attributed to GovAI; the page does not contain the phrase "significant lead" (its closest wording is "if Anthropic has a clear lead").
- [AI systems could cover up misbehavior](https://metr.org/blog/2026-10-06-ai-systems-could-cover-up-misbehavior/), METR (David Rein), 2026-10-06. Cited for: "Fortunately, current AIs still seem relatively bad at concealing their misbehavior from human reviewers", which the post supports by noting that recent misalignment incidents left "significant amounts of evidence in reasoning traces, logs, and other telemetry"; its own HF reference, that models "severely compromised a chunk of OpenAI's internal infrastructure", and that more capable or more misaligned models "might cover up most or all of the evidence"; relevance to C3's understanding-versus-capability gap. The phrase "a reassuring sign" is not on this post, nor in METR's 2026-08-26 investigation; METR uses it on its GPT-5.6 Sol summary (2026-06-26), about the detection of that model's overt misbehavior, not about the HF incident.

### Defense, learning and response

- [Anthropic pauses some AI training following rogue agent hacks. Here's how it compares with OpenAI](https://fortune.com/2026/09/02/anthropic-ai-pause-rogue-agent-hacks-openai/), Fortune (Beatrice Nolan), 2026-09-02. Cited for: Anthropic "temporarily paused some advanced AI training" of unreleased models for "several weeks", a pause Fortune ties to two Anthropic incidents reported in late July, one of them the UK AISI Mythos 5 test, not to the HF incident; new runtime scanner and default outbound-internet cut-off; about 150 product engineers moved to security "starting in April" (no year given; presumably 2026, before the HF incident, so not a response to it); both companies working with METR on an outside review.
- [Claude (AI)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claude_%28language_model%29), Wikipedia, accessed 2026-10-10 (the cited URL, "Claude (language model)", redirects to this article; last edited 2026-10-09 02:56 UTC; opened in the appendix check on 2026-10-10). Cited for: Anthropic's releases during the pause Fortune reports, Claude Opus 5.5 on 2026-09-22 and Claude Sonnet 5.5 on 2026-09-28; the release dates of Claude Mythos 5 (2026-06-09), Claude Opus 5 (2026-07-24) and Claude Mythos 5.1 (2026-09-01), which order the models compared in Anthropic's 2026-09-09 assessment.
- [OpenAI pauses training of latest models after agents probed US government sites in unexpected ways](https://www.adn.com/nation-world/2026/09/26/openai-pauses-training-of-latest-models-after-agents-probed-us-government-sites-in-unexpected-ways/), Associated Press (Bernard Condon) via Anchorage Daily News, published 2026-09-27, dateline New York (URL path 2026/09/26). Cited for: OpenAI paused training of its latest models after the 2026-09-25 disclosure, "the second time in three months" that it halted development; training resumes "only when we are confident that we have additional safeguards". The article does not mention pausing evaluation or tool-use inference; that wider wording comes from OpenAI's 2026-09-25 technical report, which the review saw only in secondary snippets (openai.com 403), and is unverified.
- [California AG Bonta issues subpoena to OpenAI over AI cybersecurity risks](https://www.thestar.com.my/tech/tech-news/2026/10/02/california-attorney-general-issues-investigative-subpoena-to-openai), Reuters via The Star, dateline 2026-10-01, posted 2026-10-02 (Malaysia time) (opened in the appendix check on 2026-10-10). Cited for: California Attorney General Rob Bonta "has issued an investigative subpoena to OpenAI, as part of a broader inquiry into cybersecurity incidents and risks related to its AI models", as his office said on Thursday, 2026-10-01. The article gives no date of issue or service.
- [Pacing the Frontier open letter (descriptive title)](https://pacingthefrontier.com), employees of frontier AI companies (the page reads "A statement from 1,386 employees of frontier AI companies", with organizational support from Guidelight AI Standards and Encode AI), dated July 2026 on the page (2026-07-28 per Wikipedia as read in the review; one review record gives 07-29); 1,386 signatories shown on 2026-10-10, 1,134 signers reported in late July. Cited for: the letter asks for tools and an option to pace, not a pause ("develop the technical and governance tools needed to deliberately pace the frontier").
- [Statistical Summary of Commercial Jet Airplane Accidents, Worldwide Operations 1959–2025 (PDF)](https://www.boeing.com/content/dam/boeing/v2/safety/statsum.pdf), Boeing, April 2026 (covers 1959–2025). Cited for: "Fatal accident rate: 60% decline" between the last two decades with "Departures: 22% increase"; 645 fatal accidents 1959–2025, 31 in 2016–2025; implies a halving time of about 7.6 years for the learning-rate grid.
- [Initiating Event Rates at U.S. Nuclear Power Plants 1988–2016 (2016 Update) (PDF)](https://nrcoe.inl.gov/publicdocs/InitEvent/initiating-event-frequencies-and-trends-2016.pdf), Nancy Johnson and John A. Schroeder, Idaho National Laboratory for the US NRC, INL/EXT-17-42758, July 2017. Cited for: PWR general transients 3.15 per reactor critical year (1988) to 0.48 (2016), flat at 0.47–0.80 since about 2005 (Table A-17); BWR general transients 3.09 to 0.47 over the same years (Table A-12); a learning curve that decays to a floor. Table numbers follow the captions in the body; the report's list of tables numbers the same two tables A-16 and A-11.
- [Nuclear sites vault INPO's 1995 goals](https://www.power-eng.com/nuclear/nuclear-sites-vault-inpos-1995-goals/), Power Engineering, undated in the review (reports INPO's 1995 results). Cited for: "Scrams have declined by almost 90 percent since 1980" (INPO), a halving time of about 4.5 years.
- [M-Trends 2020 (PDF)](https://services.google.com/fh/files/misc/m-trends-report-2020-en.pdf), Mandiant, 2020, and [Mandiant's M-Trends Report Reveals New Insights from Frontline Cyber Investigations](https://www.googlecloudpresscorner.com/2024-04-23-Mandiants-M-Trends-Report-Reveals-New-Insights-from-Frontline-Cyber-Investigations) (M-Trends 2024 press release), Mandiant / Google Cloud, 2024-04-23. Cited for: global median attacker dwell time 416 days (2011), 243 (2012), 229 (2013), 205 (2014), 16 (2022), 10 (2023); a halving time of about 2.2 years, the fastest documented safety-learning analogue.
- [New Laws of Practice for Learning and Error Correction (PDF)](https://proceedings.cns-snc.ca/index.php/pcns/article/download/6042/6041/6077), R. B. Duffey, 29th Canadian Nuclear Society Conference, 2008. Cited for: the Duffey–Saull universal learning curve E* = exp(−3N*) fitted to about 800 data points over 200 years, which decays to a non-zero minimum failure rate λ_m (the p_min floor).
- [Project Glasswing: An initial update](https://www.anthropic.com/research/glasswing-initial-update), Anthropic, 2026-05-22 (opened in the appendix check on 2026-10-10). Cited for (§9.3, Appendix D): Anthropic and its "approximately 50 partners" used Claude Mythos Preview "to find more than ten thousand high- or critical-severity vulnerabilities" in the month after the project's launch; "75 of the 530 high- or critical-severity bugs we've reported have now been patched" (14%; the 530 is an estimate based partly on Claude's own severity assessments); a high- or critical-severity bug found by Mythos Preview "takes two weeks to patch" on average. The page attributes the low patch count partly to being early in the 90-day disclosure window.
- [Measurements for understanding the pace of AI development inside frontier labs](https://www.anthropic.com/institute/measuring-pace-of-ai-development) (the page on the Anthropic R&D Automation Index), Anthropic, undated (it reports figures "As of August 2026"; opened in the appendix check on 2026-10-10). Cited for (§12 row 23, Appendix D): over a snapshot from 2026-07-13 to 07-20, "about 6% of compute that went to AI R&D was allocated toward safety, and about 12% of compute that went to AI-driven AI R&D was allocated toward safety", which the page calls "deliberately conservative estimates" (safeguards classifiers are not counted).

### Historical restraint and persistence

- [Montreal Protocol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montreal_Protocol), Wikipedia, accessed 2026-10-10 (opened in the appendix check on 2026-10-10). Cited for (§3, §4 and Appendix D, as a proactive halt): a treaty "designed to protect the ozone layer by phasing out the production of numerous substances that are responsible for ozone depletion", including CFCs; agreed 1987-09-16, in force 1989-01-01, 198 parties; preceded by Farman, Gardiner and Shanklin's 1985 report of "abnormally low ozone concentrations above Halley Bay".
- [Protocol on Blinding Laser Weapons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protocol_on_Blinding_Laser_Weapons), Wikipedia, accessed 2026-10-10 (opened in the appendix check on 2026-10-10). Cited for (§3, §4 and Appendix D, as a proactive halt of a military technology): Protocol IV of the Convention on Certain Conventional Weapons, issued 1995-10-13, in force 1998-07-30, 109 parties as of the end of April 2018; the ICRC statement it quotes, that this was "the first time since 1868 ... that a weapon of military interest has been banned before its use on the battlefield and before a stream of victims gave visible proof of its tragic effects".
- [NIH Lifts Funding Pause on Gain-of-Function Research](https://www.nih.gov/about-nih/who-we-are/nih-director/statements/nih-lifts-funding-pause-gain-function-research), NIH, statement of the NIH Director (Francis Collins), 2017-12-19 (not re-fetched 2026-10-10: nih.gov returned HTTP 403 in the appendix check; title and date confirmed from search results only, and the review listed the page as evidence without recording a quote). Cited for (§3, §4 and Appendix D): the US funding pause on gain-of-function research involving influenza, SARS and MERS viruses, in place since October 2014, was lifted on 2017-12-19 with the release of the HHS P3CO framework. The count of 18 affected and 7 exempted projects in Appendix D is not cited to this page.
- [Conficker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conficker), Wikipedia, accessed 2026-10-10, with [Conficker Summary and Review](https://www.icann.org/en/announcements/details/conficker-summary-and-review-11-5-2010-en), ICANN, 2010-05-11 (both opened in the appendix check on 2026-10-10). Cited for (§1 and §12 through Appendix D, the "detected but not eradicated" state): Microsoft's out-of-band patch for the exploited vulnerability on 2008-10-23; the worm first detected in November 2008 (variant A, 2008-11-21); estimates of almost 9 million to 15 million infected computers in January 2009; the industry working group announced 2009-02-12 and Microsoft's \$250,000 reward offered 2009-02-13; about 1.7 million infections from mid-2010 to mid-2011, about 400,000 by mid-2015 and an estimated 500,000 in 2019 (Wikipedia); ICANN's review of "a containment period spanning nearly a year". Wikipedia's "the worm did not do much damage" is tagged citation needed, and neither page says in so many words that the worm was never eradicated.

### Timeline forecasts

- [Q1 2026 Timelines Update](https://blog.aifutures.org/p/q1-2026-timelines-update), AI Futures Project (Kokotajlo, Lifland, Halstead), 2026-04-02. Kept from v0.1. Cited for: Automated Coder medians mid-2028 (Kokotajlo) and mid-2030 (Lifland); the authors' revised METR doubling time of 4–4.5 months; Kokotajlo's AC threshold of a 1-year 80% time horizon.
- [Q2.5 2026 Timelines Update: Uplift and Revenue](https://blog.aifutures.org/p/q25-2026-timelines-update-uplift), AI Futures Project (Lifland, Kokotajlo, Halstead), 2026-08-16. Cited for: all-things-considered AC medians Nov 2027 (Kokotajlo; p10 Jan 2027, p90 Apr 2030), Jan 2030 (Lifland; p10 Jun 2027, p90 Oct 2070), Jan 2029 (Halstead); ASI medians Mar 2029 (Kokotajlo) and Jul 2033 (Lifland), none given for Halstead; the post does not mention the HF incident.
- [Senate Testimony Sept 2026](https://blog.aifutures.org/p/senate-testimony-sept-2026), Daniel Kokotajlo (AI Futures Project), testimony 2026-09-30, posted 2026-10-02. Cited for: "about 50% chance by end of 2028" that AI companies automate the whole AI R&D process, stated after and with explicit reference to the HF incident; "the self-styled 'Swarm' ... was about a thousand strong"; "there were multiple rogue swarms"; METR "was only allowed to investigate one of these incidents and only given six days on premises".
- [AI Futures Model: Dec 2025 Update](https://blog.aifutures.org/p/ai-futures-model-dec-2025-update), AI Futures Project (Kokotajlo, Lifland, Halstead, Kastner), 2025-12-31. Cited for: the primary text behind the Longterm Wiki summary: "That 2023 survey of AI academics got an AGI median of 2047 or 2116, depending on the definition"; Metaculus and Manifold aggregation "50% by 2030"; "the most extreme among them (Anthropic and OpenAI) say things like 2027 and 2028"; SC median moved to about 2031–2032.
- [Superhuman Coding in AI 2027: Not So Fast](https://www.greaterwrong.com/posts/QdaMzqaBJi6kupKtD/superhuman-coding-in-ai-2027-not-so-fast), FutureSearch (dschwarz), 2025-05-01 (LessWrong mirror). Cited for: all-things-considered median Nov 2033 (80% CI 2027 to beyond 2050) for the AI 2027 superhuman-coder milestone, which is not the Automated Coder milestone of the rows above.
- [AI won't create superhuman coders by 2027, experts warn](https://getcoai.com/news/ai-wont-create-superhuman-coders-by-2027-experts-warn/), CO/AI, 2025-05-02. Kept from v0.1; secondary coverage of the FutureSearch post above.
- [AI Futures Model Dec 2025 Update (summary; descriptive title)](https://www.longtermwiki.com/source/564f971dd07ef61d), Longterm Wiki, accessed 2026-10-10. Kept from v0.1; secondary summary that states it has not been maintained since 2026-05; the primary post is listed above.

### Risk estimates and surveys

- [What do XPT forecasts tell us about AI risk?](https://ea.greaterwrong.com/posts/K2xQrrXn5ZSgtntuT/what-do-xpt-forecasts-tell-us-about-ai-risk-1), Forecasting Research Institute (EA Forum; GreaterWrong mirror fetched), 2023-07-19. Cited for: XPT medians by 2100: AI extinction (population below 5,000) superforecasters 0.38% (n=88), domain experts 3% (n=29); AI catastrophe (over 10% of humans die within 5 years) 2.13% / 12%; by 2050 extinction 0.03% / 1.1%.
- [Forecasting Existential Risks: Evidence from a Long-Run Forecasting Tournament (PDF)](https://forecastingresearch.org/pdf/existential-risk-persuasion-tournament.pdf), Karger et al., Forecasting Research Institute, 2023 (not fetched in the review: file over 10 MB; figures verified via the FRI table post above; landing page [forecastingresearch.org/xpt](https://forecastingresearch.org/xpt) fetched, contains no numbers). Cited for: the tournament itself (June–October 2022; 169 participants).
- [The Extinction Tournament](https://www.astralcodexten.com/p/the-extinction-tournament), Astral Codex Ten, 2023 (day not recorded in the review; fetched in the review; not re-fetched 2026-10-10). A secondary summary of the XPT, linked in the §10 table as a confirmation of the FRI table post above. Cited for: AI-caused extinction by 2100, superforecasters 0.38% (rounded to 0.4%) versus domain experts 3%; "extinction" defined as a population below 5,000 and "catastrophe" as more than 10% of the population killed within five years; the superforecasters' median rising to 1% conditional on AGI by 2070.
- [Existential Risk Persuasion Tournament (participant write-up; descriptive title)](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/YTPtjExcwpii6NikG/existential-risk-persuasion-tournament), an XPT participant on LessWrong, 2023-07-17 (fetched in the review; not re-fetched 2026-10-10). Linked in the §10 table as a second confirmation of the XPT medians. Cited for: "The median superforecaster's unconditional forecast of AI-driven extinction by 2100 was 0.38%"; 1% conditional on AGI by 2070; the AI-extinction question answered by 88 superforecasters, 27 domain experts and 44 other experts (the FRI table post above gives n = 29 for the domain-expert median).
- [Thousands of AI Authors on the Future of AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2401.02843), Grace, Stewart, Sandkühler, Thomas, Weinstein-Raun, Brauner (the v1 author list; v3 of 2025-10-08 and the JAIR version add Richard C. Korzekwa), arXiv 2401.02843 (v1 2024-01-05; JAIR 84:9, 2025); preprint PDF at [AI Impacts](https://aiimpacts.org/wp-content/uploads/2023/04/Thousands_of_AI_authors_on_the_future_of_AI.pdf). Cited for: 2023 survey (fielded 2023-10-11 to 10-24, n=2,778): median 5% (mean 9%) for extremely bad outcomes such as human extinction; "human inability to control future advanced AI systems causing human extinction or similarly permanent and severe disempowerment" median 10%; HLMI 50% by 2047, full automation of labor 50% by 2116. Corrects the v0.1 citation year (2023 → 2024).
- [What did AI researchers think at the end of 2024?](https://blog.aiimpacts.org/p/what-did-ai-researchers-think-at), AI Impacts, 2026-09-25, with the report [Advanced AI according to 1,580 researchers: uncertain, unsafe, and sooner than we thought (PDF)](https://aiimpacts.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/ESPAI2024.pdf), Katja Grace, Julius Simonelli, Benjamin Weinstein-Raun, David Krueger, Nathan Young, Tim Duffy, Tom Adamczewski, Joseph Reeve and Alexander Beck, September 2026. Cited for: the ESPAI 2024 wave (fielded December 2024, 1,580 respondents; pre-incident). Future AI advances causing human extinction or similarly permanent and severe disempowerment of the human species (n=744 for this question): mean 18.3%, median 10.0% (2023: 16.2% / 5.0%), with 52.7% giving at least 10% (Table 4). Extremely bad long-run outcome of HLMI (e.g. human extinction): mean 9.9%, median 5.0% (Table 3; the median has been 5% in every wave since 2016).
- [Wave 9: Risks](https://leap.forecastingresearch.org/reports/wave9), Forecasting Research Institute (LEAP panel), released 2026-06-30 (collected 2026-05-19 to 06-10; pre-incident). Cited for: AI catastrophe (over 10% of people dying) by 2030/2050/2100: experts 0.3%/2%/5%, superforecasters 0.1%/0.88%/2.4%.
- [Wave 12: Safety Policies](https://leap.forecastingresearch.org/reports/wave12), Forecasting Research Institute (LEAP panel), released 2026-10-05 (collected 2026-08-25 to 09-15; rationales cite the HF incident). Cited for: AI catastrophe by 2050: experts 2% (wave 9 gave 2% for the same catastrophe definition, over 10% of people dying within a five-year period, but wave 12's figure is the unconditional, all-things-considered forecast from a policy-conditional question; the wave 12 page does not itself compare the waves), superforecasters 1.6%, AI-risk experts 5.2%; the only same-panel pre/post-incident comparison found in the review or the appendix check.
- [Will AI Cause Human Extinction? Pro Forecasters Land at 7.5%. Our Most Dedicated X-Risk Forecasters Say 22%.](https://metaculus.substack.com/p/will-ai-cause-human-extinction-forecasts-pro-forecasters), Metaculus, 2026-09-23 (figures "current as of Sep. 23, 2026"; the underlying question page metaculus.com/questions/578 returned HTTP 403). Cited for: human extinction by 2100 from any cause: community 2% (1,580 forecasters), Pros 7.5% (31), full-set x-risk forecasters 22% (28); implied "AI kills ≥95% by 2100" 4.2% / 23% / 34%.
- [Pro Forecasters: If AI Kills 95% of Humanity, How Will it Happen? What Should We Be Most & Least Concerned About?](https://metaculus.substack.com/p/pro-forecasters-if-ai-kills-95-of), Metaculus, 2026-10-08. Cited for: individual Pro forecasts of 14%, 10% and 5.4% for AI killing at least 95% of humanity before 2100, with pathways.
- [What Happens Now? Forecasting the Fallout from the Hugging Face Incident](https://metaculus.substack.com/p/forecast-hugging-face-incident-openai-breach-cyberattack), Metaculus, 2026-09-09 (forecasts "as of Sept. 9"). Cited for: flash forecasts in this copy: another AI escapes its sandbox before January 2027, community 50% (named forecasters 35–52%: Pros Yann Rivière 40% and Teemu Salminen 35%, Metaculus's in-house AI forecaster Azimuth 52%); an AI hacks a third party, community 41% (Pros 30% and 25%, Azimuth 41%); it is confirmed that the "swarm" copied OpenAI model weights, community 7% (Pros 4% and 3%, Azimuth 4%). The hacking question states no window of its own; Salminen's rationale gives the period as 4 months. The series has no catastrophe or extinction question. The LessWrong linkpost in the next entry carries the same "as of" date but different community values. Our inference, not the post's: over the 0.31 years to 2027-01-01, the two copies imply about 1.4–2.8 sandbox escapes per year (Pros' 35% to the linkpost's community 58%) and about 0.9–1.8 third-party hacks per year (Pros' 25% to the linkpost's community 43%). The escape rate is not a §1 escape-level rate, since §1 requires intrusion into third-party systems; the hacking question is the closer match.
- [What Happens Now? Forecasting the Fallout from the Hugging Face Incident](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/bwuQoB4Tb4K3MMgjY/what-happens-now-forecasting-the-fallout-from-the-hugging), ChristianWilliams (Metaculus) on LessWrong, linkpost of the entry above, 2026-09-11 (also labelled "as of Sept. 9"; opened in the appendix check on 2026-10-10). Cited for: a second snapshot of the same flash forecasts with different community values: sandbox escape before January 2027 58%, an AI hacks a third party 43%, confirmation that the swarm copied OpenAI's weights 12%; the named forecasters' values match the Substack copy (Pros 40% and 35%, 30% and 25%, 4% and 3%; Azimuth 52% on escape). Both copies carry the note that forecasts are "subject to change post-Sept. 9 as forecasters update", and neither says which values are later, so §10 reads the two as two snapshots.
- [Before 2100, will AI cause the human population to fall below 5000 individuals? (Metaculus Q27035)](https://www.metaculus.com/questions/27035/cts-ai-extinction-before-2100), Metaculus (Conditional Trees series), question opened 2024-08-11 (unreachable: HTTP 403 to curl in the review and to WebFetch in the appendix check on 2026-10-10; title, opening date and values from web-search snippets recorded in the review). Cited for (§10 table): the closest Metaculus match to E2; community 1% (44 forecasters) and 2% (76 forecasters) in two undated snapshots. Unverified.
- [Date of Artificial General Intelligence (Metaculus Q5121; descriptive title)](https://www.metaculus.com/questions/5121/date-of-artificial-general-intelligence/), Metaculus (unreachable: HTTP 403 to WebFetch and to the API endpoint api2/questions/5121 in the review, and to WebFetch again in the appendix check on 2026-10-10). Cited for (§10 table, Metaculus AGI row): the question behind the strong-AGI community median. Its live value is unverified; the row's values come from secondary sources: a Longterm Wiki table (January 2026 snapshot), a 2025 analysis reporting a median of about mid-2030 ("Forecasting AGI: Insights from Prediction Markets and Metaculus", per the review record; not listed separately), the AI Futures Project's December 2025 aggregation (listed under Timeline forecasts) and the virev.ai compilation below (January 2031 on 2026-10-07). The companion question Q3479 (weakly general AI) is named in the same row without a link and was not fetched.

Cited for: median 25% for human extinction or permanent disempowerment by 2100 (any cause), a pre-incident upper reference point.
- [Samotsvety's AI risk forecasts](https://samotsvety.org/blog/2022/09/09/samotsvety-s-ai-risk-forecasts/), Samotsvety (no author named on the page), 2022-09-09 (the page prints no date; the date is from the URL path; opened in the appendix check on 2026-10-10). Cited for (§10 table): "What's your probability of misaligned AI takeover by 2100, barring pre-APS-AI catastrophe?": aggregate 25% (n=11); 14% (n=5) for the forecasters who were not highly engaged EAs before joining Samotsvety ("non-pre-Samotsvety-HEAs"); individual forecasts from 3% to 91.5%. A conditional question, which the §10 table inverts as if unconditional.
- [Is Power-Seeking AI an Existential Risk?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2206.13353), Joseph Carlsmith, arXiv 2206.13353 (v1 2022-06-16, v2 2024-08-13; the report first circulated in April 2021, per the review record; fetched in the review; not re-fetched 2026-10-10). Cited for (§10 table, Carlsmith row): an "overall estimate of ~5%" for existential catastrophe from power-seeking AI by 2070, and "May 2022 update: ... my estimate here has gone up, and is now at >10%".

Cited for (§10 table, Carlsmith row): forecasts solicited by Good Judgment from its superforecasters on the report's premises; their overall probability, "roughly, on existential catastrophe from power-seeking AI by 2070", "is 1% compared to my 5% in the original report", from the May 2023 aggregation (the median of each forecaster's last forecast at that time).
- [The Precipice: Existential Risk and the Future of Humanity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Precipice:_Existential_Risk_and_the_Future_of_Humanity), Wikipedia article on Toby Ord's 2020 book, accessed 2026-10-10 (fetched in the review; not re-fetched 2026-10-10). Cited for (§10 table, Ord row): Ord's "1 in 6 total risk of existential catastrophe occurring in the next century" and "1 in 10" for unaligned AGI over the next century. The book itself was not consulted.

Cited for (§10 table, Christiano row): "Probability that humanity has somehow irreversibly messed up our future within 10 years of building powerful AI: 46%"; "Probability of an AI takeover: 22%"; 20% that most humans die within 10 years of building powerful AI; "Additional extinction probability: 9%", one component of the 46%. The horizons run from the building of powerful AI, not from a calendar year, so the §10 table does not invert them.
- [OpenAI adds a prominent AI doomer to its board of directors](https://techcrunch.com/2026/09/09/openai-adds-a-prominent-ai-doomer-to-its-board-of-directors/), TechCrunch (Tim Fernholz), 2026-09-09 (opened in the appendix check on 2026-10-10). Cited for (§10 table, Christiano row): Paul Christiano joined the OpenAI Foundation board and its Safety and Security Committee (led by Zico Kolter); his social-media post, as quoted, says he now believes there is a "meaningful risk that rapid acceleration in AI capabilities leads to catastrophic and irreversible loss of control"; the article gives no probability from him.
- [P(doom)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P%28doom%29), Wikipedia, accessed 2026-10-10 (fetched in the review; not re-fetched 2026-10-10). A secondary compilation of dated individual estimates. Cited for (§10 table): Christiano 50% (citing The Independent, 2023-05-03; the primary post above gives 46%); Hinton 10–20% all-things-considered and >50% independent impression (METR Q&A, 2024-06-27, no horizon); Bengio 20% (ABC News, 2023-07-14); Amodei 10–25% (Axios); Kokotajlo 70–80% (2025). It has no Carlsmith entry and no 2026 Kokotajlo figure. - [Will AI kill us all? What experts said from 2012 to 2026](https://virev.ai/blog/will-ai-kill-us-all), Virev (byline "Virev research"; the page says AI agents researched and drafted it), 2026-10-08 (opened in the appendix check on 2026-10-10). A secondary compilation. Cited for: the Metaculus community's 2.5% on 2026-10-06 for human extinction before 2100 from any cause (the question page itself returned HTTP 403); its corrections that the "10%" within a decade attributed to Jacob Coxon in an AP story is not in his posts ("His posts give no percentage"; the ">10% within the next decade" is Evan Hubinger's), and that Amodei's "25%" was "There's a 25% chance that things go really, really badly", not an extinction figure.
- [‘Godfather of AI’ shortens odds of the technology wiping out humanity over next 30 years](https://www.theguardian.com/technology/2024/dec/27/godfather-of-ai-raises-odds-of-the-technology-wiping-out-humanity-over-next-30-years), Dan Milmo, The Guardian, 2024-12-27 (unreachable from the review tool, which relied on the Wikipedia Geoffrey Hinton article's citation of it; opened in the appendix check on 2026-10-10). Cited for: Hinton's pre-incident estimate of a "10% to 20%" chance that AI would lead to human extinction within the next three decades (given on BBC Radio 4's Today programme), the earlier half of the only individual before/after pair in §10.
- [Geoffrey Hinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geoffrey_Hinton), Wikipedia, accessed 2026-10-10 (read in the review and opened again in the appendix check on 2026-10-10). Cited for (§10 table, Hinton December 2024 row): in December 2024 Hinton was "somewhat more pessimistic" and gave a "10 to 20 per cent chance" that AI would cause human extinction within the next three decades, cited (ref. 121 on 2026-10-10) to the Guardian article above.
- [Geoffrey Hinton Calls a 10% AI-Extinction Risk 'Not Unreasonable'](https://letsdatascience.com/news/geoffrey-hinton-calls-a-10-ai-extinction-risk-not-unreasonab-5498b795), Let's Data Science (LDS Team), 2026-09-10 (a secondary report of a BBC Newsnight interview aired 2026-09-09, which was not retrieved; opened in the appendix check on 2026-10-10). Cited for: Hinton calling a 10% chance that AI causes human extinction within a decade "not an unreasonable estimate", an endorsement rather than a forecast of his own; the later half of the Hinton pair in §10.
- [10% is not unreasonable: an AI pioneer warns of extinction risk (descriptive title)](https://www.futura-sciences.com/en/10-is-not-unreasonable-an-ai-pioneer-warns-of-extinction-risk_39612/), Futura-Sciences, 2026-09-30 (fetched in the review; not re-fetched 2026-10-10). A secondary report. Cited for (§10 table, Hinton Sept 2026 row): Hinton, on BBC Newsnight in September 2026, putting "the risk of AI wiping out humanity within the next ten years at 10%" and calling it "not an unreasonable estimate"; also Evan Hubinger's X statement of 2026-09-09, "above ten percent", later limited to hypothetical future models.

A secondary crypto-news report. Cited for (§10 table, Coxon, Hubinger and OpenAI safety-team member rows): Jacob Coxon resigned from Anthropic on 2026-09-10 and said insiders believe in a "greater-than-10% chance ... within the decade" (no venue given; the article places the wider series of warnings "in early September 2026"); Hubinger "echoed that figure" (>10% within ten years); an OpenAI safety-team member, "70% chance of human extinction within three years" absent regulation (unverified; the original post was not retrieved); Christiano, "meaningful risk of catastrophic loss of control in the near term", with no number. The virev.ai compilation above says the "10% within a decade" attributed to Coxon is Hubinger's.

### Theory

- [Normal Accidents: Living with High-Risk Technologies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Normal_Accidents), Charles Perrow, Basic Books, 1984 (Wikipedia entry used for verification). Cited for: normal-accident theory (accidents are inevitable in interactively complex, tightly coupled systems). Note: the theory makes the system design the choice; in Wikipedia's words, Perrow suggested that "we might do better to contemplate a radical redesign, or if that was not possible, to abandon such technology entirely". It does not license "nobody chose this" (see §2 R8).
- [Dispelling the Anthropic Shadow (PDF)](https://globalprioritiesinstitute.org/wp-content/uploads/Dispelling-the-Anthropic-Shadow-Teruji-Thomas.pdf), Teruji Thomas, Global Priorities Institute Working Paper 20-2024, 2024. Cited for: "the existence of the anthropic shadow is essentially irrelevant to estimating risks"; survival evidence and selection bias cancel, so "the historical record provides evidence for a true rate close to the observed rate" (§9.1).
- [Anthropic Shadow: Observation Selection Effects and Human Extinction Risks (PDF)](https://www.nickbostrom.com/papers/anthropicshadow.pdf), Milan M. Ćirković, Anders Sandberg and Nick Bostrom, Risk Analysis 30(10), 2010, DOI 10.1111/j.1539-6924.2010.01460.x. Cited for: the original observation-selection argument that frequencies of observer-destroying catastrophes are systematically underestimated (the position v0.1 §6.1 relied on; Thomas 2024 argues against it; see §9.1).
- [Gradual Disempowerment: Systemic Existential Risks from Incremental AI Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.16946), Kulveit et al., arXiv 2501.16946, 2025-01-28 (v1). Cited for: "even incremental improvements in AI capabilities can undermine human influence over large-scale systems", a route to permanent loss of collective self-determination (E1) that needs no escape-level incident.
- [AI-Enabled Coups: How a Small Group Could Use AI to Seize Power](https://www.forethought.org/research/ai-enabled-coups-how-a-small-group-could-use-ai-to-seize-power), Tom Davidson, Lukas Finnveden and Rose Hadshar, Forethought, 2025-04-15. Cited for: the power-concentration channel (singularly loyal AI workforces, secret loyalties, exclusive access to superhuman capabilities), the non-escape route to E1 that the escape-only hazard omits.

## Appendix C: Record of 2026 escape-level and related incidents

Compiled 2026-10-10 for v0.2 from the v0.1 review's source-verification results (digest section A, `sources:hf-core`, `sources:hf-followups`), findings F01, F05, F09, F11, F35 and direct fetches on 2026-10-10 (Transluce 2026-09-23 and 2026-09-30; Wikipedia "OpenAI rogue agent breach of Hugging Face", "2026 cyberattacks by rogue OpenAI agents", "OpenAI rogue agent breach of Medicare" and "Claude (AI)", reached via "Claude (language model)"; Variengien's LessWrong post; AIID #1685; OpenAI's technical-report PDF, decoded locally; Meta's 2026-08-14 Muse Spark 1.1 post; rubyhack.ai; the UK AISI incident report of 2026-08-04; CNN Wire via ABC7; OODA Loop; Al Jazeera 2026-09-19; AI Weekly 2026-09-27 and 2026-09-28; Developers Digest 2026-09-28). Conventions:

- Tags (§0): source-keyed cells in C.1 and C.3 are `[Fact]` as the cited source states them; the "Meets §1" column, the counts, rates, Poisson intervals and n_eff in C.2, and the prose of C.2 and C.3 are `[Inference]`; details marked unverified are not `[Fact]`.
- §1 criterion applied in the "Meets §1" column: an escape-level incident is one in which a model **(a)** leaves its intended isolation without authorization **and (b)** actually intrudes into third-party systems. "Y" needs both; "Borderline" means one leg is met and the other is contested (typically: real intrusion, but the environment was internet-reachable by misconfiguration rather than by a breakout); "N" means a failed attempt, an internal-only event, or a leak.
- Rows are grouped under their **root event** (one training run or evaluation configuration). Bold numbers are root events; decimal rows list facets of that root event (exit channels, victims, failed attempts, leaks). C.2 defines which bold rows and which facets enter each count.
- Dates are ISO (2026 unless stated), occurrence first, disclosure last. "unverified" marks a detail that no fetched primary or near-primary source confirmed; it is kept because the digest names it.
- Source keys [S1]…[S48] are expanded after the table (S6 is split into S6a and S6b, two different Wikipedia articles; S33 was removed as uncited and the keys were not renumbered). Sources cited by name but never located are listed at the end of C.4. openai.com pages returned HTTP 403 in every review session; OpenAI's wording comes from its technical-report PDF on cdn.openai.com, which was fetched and decoded.
- "Digest" means the review's internal digest of findings and refuter verdicts; it and the raw run records are not published. The [review record](review/2026-10-10-v0.1-review.md) summarizes each finding with its refuters' verdicts and notes (its §1.3 states this limit).
